To check (4)

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 5 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

p = 0.40 Women had a higher prevalence than men in every cohort (odds ratios for men 0.62-0.77), with no evidence of cohort heterogeneity in that pattern (interaction p = 0.40; Q = 2.93, I² = 0).
p = 0.40 Heterogeneity was not detected by the pre-specified random-effects analysis (I² = 0.0%, p = 0.40), although label × cohort interaction tests ranged from p = 0.004 to p = 0.47 across specifications; the relative risk attached to the label is therefore broadly similar across cohorts even where its abs…

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. setwd() calls were found in 9 code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In 1 code file, libraries/imports were at multiple places (i.e., with more than 3 non-comment lines in between).

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 21 distinct packages/libraries: data.table, dplyr, ggplot2, grDevices, gridExtra, haven, lavaan, MASS, mice, mirt, patchwork, pdftools, pROC, ragg, scales, semTools, sirt, survey, survival, systemfonts, tidyr. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

Reproducible Environment

No renv.lock file, sessionInfo()/session_info() record, or groundhog/checkpoint date-pin call was found anywhere in the repository. Without one of these, the exact R and package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and package versions may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. Consider depositing an renv.lock (via the renv package), a sessionInfo() text dump, or a groundhog/checkpoint date-pin alongside the code.

Careless Responding

No file could be screened for careless responding.

What this does not cover. Only 0 of 91 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

File paths

48 data paths in the code do not work in the repository as deposited. We pointed them to where the file is, so the code could run: out_ci.csv to ../derived/out_ci.csv (in 103_01_hard.R); out_spec_curve.csv to ../derived/out_spec_curve.csv (in 103_01_hard.R); out_std_who.csv to ../derived/out_std_who.csv (in 103_01_hard.R); out_dif.csv to ../derived/out_dif.csv (in 61_04_mirt.R); out_irt_latentmeans.csv to ../derived/out_irt_latentmeans.csv (in 61_05_fig.R); out_irt_mirt_params.csv to ../derived/out_irt_mirt_params.csv (in 61_05_fig.R); F:/JCSM/out_ci.csv to ../derived/out_ci.csv (in 67_00_dbg.R); F:/JCSM to ../derived (in 67_01_ci.R); F:/JCSM to ../derived (in 67_02_share_sens.R); F:/JCSM/72_00_funcs.R to 72_00_funcs.R (in 72_02_thresholds.R), and 38 more. The code should be changed, because as written it does not find these files. The code reads files that are only inside derived_data_and_code_v1.zip, and no script unpacks it. We unpacked it in its folder before the run. So that others can run the code as deposited, start the code with a line that unpacks it (such as unzip("derived_data_and_code_v1.zip") in R), or upload the files unpacked. 19 data paths were built at runtime with sprintf()/paste()/file.path() instead of written as a literal path — this is fragile authoring practice (it silently breaks if the code is run from a different working directory than the author’s own). We resolved all of these on a best-effort basis.

Execution

The code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 91 scripts: 13 ran without error, 73 errored, 0 timed out, 0 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 1 did not parse, and 4 were run by another script with source(). Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions.

setwd() in the code

9 scripts call setwd(). This is bad practice: it hardcodes where the code must run (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine) and breaks the moment the code is run anywhere else. To run the code we replaced these calls with a statement that does nothing. Any such script that then ran is reproducible only after ignoring a setwd() that should not have been in the code and needs to be fixed.

Reproducible after removing setwd(): 84_04_fig.R, 87_01_impossibility.R, 88_01_hcap.R.

Run by another script

4 scripts are run by another script with source(), so they were not run on their own: each script runs in a fresh R process, and on their own a sourced script lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first. Their outcome is part of the sourcing script’s outcome.

> n2 <- function(x) { x <- as.numeric(unclass(x)); x[!is.na(x) & x < 0] <- NA; x }

> YEAR <- list(

> build_wave <- function(cohort, wave, file, vars) {
>   d <- as.data.frame(read_dta(file, col_select = all_of(unique(c("rabyear","ragender", vars)))))
>   w <- wave
>   pre <- paste0("r", w)
>   is_hrs <- cohort == "HRS"
>   o <- data.frame(
>     cohort = cohort, wave = as.integer(w), year = YEAR[[cohort]][[as.character(w)]],
>     id = if ("hhidpn" %in% names(d)) n2(d$hhidpn) else
>          if ("idauniq" %in% names(d)) n2(d$idauniq) else
>          if ("mergeid" %in% names(d)) as.character(d$mergeid) else
>          if ("ID" %in% names(d)) n2(d$ID) else seq_len(nrow(d)),
>     age = YEAR[[cohort]][[as.character(w)]] - n2(d$rabyear),
>     sex = n2(d$ragender),
>     iw  = pick(d, paste0(pre, "iwstat")),

>     bwc20 = pick(d, paste0(pre, "bwc20")),
>     proxy = if (paste0(pre, "proxy") %in% names(d)) as.numeric(unclass(d[[paste0(pre, "proxy")]])) %in% 1 else FALSE,
>     prmem = pick(d, paste0(pre, "prmem")),
>     iadl  = if (paste0(pre, "iadl5a") %in% names(d)) pick(d, paste0(pre, "iadl5a")) else
>             if (paste0(pre, "iadlfour") %in% names(d)) pick(d, paste0(pre, "iadlfour")) else NA_real_,
>     iwy   = pick(d, paste0(pre, "iwy")),

>     o$edu3 <- n2(d$raeducl)

>   key <- as.integer((M - 1L) %*% mult)
>   u <- sort(unique(key)); idx <- match(key, u)
>   list(key = key, u = u, idx = idx, S = length(u), n = tabulate(idx, nbins = length(u)))

> lca_fit <- function(tab, Jmax, K, starts = 10, tol = 1e-10, maxiter = 8000, seed = 1,
>                     init = NULL, jitter = 0) {
>   if (!is.null(seed)) set.seed(seed); S <- tab$S; N <- sum(tab$n); J <- length(Jmax)
>   pat <- matrix(0L, S, J)
>   for (j in seq_len(J)) pat[, j] <- ((tab$u %/% cumprod(c(1, Jmax[-J]))[j]) %% Jmax[j]) + 1L
>   best <- NULL
>   for (st in seq_len(max(1, starts))) {
>     if (!is.null(init)) {
>       pi <- if (jitter > 0) as.numeric(rgamma(K, 1 + init$pi * jitter)) else init$pi
>       pi <- pi / sum(pi)
>       th <- lapply(seq_len(J), function(j) {
>         m <- if (jitter > 0) matrix(rgamma(K * Jmax[j], 1 + init$theta[[j]] * jitter), K, Jmax[j])
>              else init$theta[[j]]
>         m / rowSums(m) })
>     } else {
>       pi <- as.numeric(rgamma(K, 1)); pi <- pi / sum(pi)
>       th <- lapply(Jmax, function(Kj) { m <- matrix(rgamma(K * Kj, 1), K, Kj); m / rowSums(m) })
>     }
>     ll_old <- -Inf
>     for (it in seq_len(maxiter)) {
>       lw <- matrix(log(pmax(pi, 1e-300)), S, K, byrow = TRUE)
>       for (j in seq_len(J)) lw <- lw + t(log(pmax(th[[j]], 1e-300))[, pat[, j], drop = FALSE])
>       mx <- apply(lw, 1, max); lse <- mx + log(rowSums(exp(lw - mx)))
>       ll <- sum(tab$n * lse)
>       r <- exp(lw - lse); Nsk <- r * tab$n
>       pi <- colSums(Nsk) / N
>       for (j in seq_len(J)) {
>         for (c in seq_len(Jmax[j])) th[[j]][, c] <- colSums(Nsk[pat[, j] == c, , drop = FALSE])
>         th[[j]] <- th[[j]] / pmax(rowSums(th[[j]]), 1e-300)
>       }
>       if (ll - ll_old < tol * (1 + abs(ll))) break
>       ll_old <- ll
>     }
>     if (is.null(best) || ll > best$llik + 1e-8)
>       best <- list(llik = ll, pi = pi, theta = th, it = it, K = K, Jmax = Jmax)
>   }
>   lw <- matrix(log(pmax(best$pi, 1e-300)), S, K, byrow = TRUE)
>   for (j in seq_len(J)) lw <- lw + t(log(pmax(best$theta[[j]], 1e-300))[, pat[, j], drop = FALSE])
>   mx <- apply(lw, 1, max); lse <- mx + log(rowSums(exp(lw - mx)))
>   best$post <- exp(lw - lse)
>   best$npar <- (K - 1) + sum(K * (Jmax - 1))
>   best$N <- N; best$ncells <- prod(Jmax); best$df <- prod(Jmax) - 1 - best$npar
>   best$AIC <- -2 * best$llik + 2 * best$npar
>   best$BIC <- -2 * best$llik + best$npar * log(N)
>   best$aBIC <- -2 * best$llik + best$npar * log((N + 2) / 24)
>   p <- best$post; p <- p / pmax(rowSums(p), 1e-300)
>   best$entropy <- 1 + sum(p * log(pmax(p, 1e-300))) / (N * log(K))
>   best
> }

> ratio_ci <- function(p1, s1, p2, s2) {           # p1/p2，独立样本，log 尺度 delta
>   p1 <- unname(p1); s1 <- unname(s1); p2 <- unname(p2); s2 <- unname(s2)
>   lr <- log(p1 / p2); v <- (s1 / p1)^2 + (s2 / p2)^2
>   c(est = exp(lr), lo = exp(lr - 1.96 * sqrt(v)), hi = exp(lr + 1.96 * sqrt(v)))
> }
> diff_ci <- function(p1, s1, p2, s2) {
>   p1 <- unname(p1); s1 <- unname(s1); p2 <- unname(p2); s2 <- unname(s2)
>   d <- p1 - p2; s <- sqrt(s1^2 + s2^2)
>   c(est = d, lo = d - 1.96 * s, hi = d + 1.96 * s)

More detail

Ethics Approval Check

The paper does not appear to report data about individual people or animals (we found: figures about groups or things), so we do not expect an ethics statement.

We based this on:

The Global Burden of Disease (GBD) study estimated 57.4 million people living with dementia in 2019, rising to 152.8 million by 2050, with an agestandardised prevalence that barely changes (+0.1%) -a projection driven almost entirely by population ageing [1].

Eighth, misclassification risk in algorithmic classification is not random: it has been shown to vary systematically with race and ethnicity in the USA [29], and our own results show the same for education, so the country-level bias we document is likely to be heterogeneous within countries as well.

Ethics statement:

consent: the Health and Retirement Study by the University of Michigan Institutional Review Board; the English Longitudinal Study of Ageing, including the ELSA-HCAP wave, by the UK National Research Ethics Service; the Survey of Health, Ageing and Retirement in Europe by the Ethics Council of the Max Planck Society together with national committees in the participating countries; and the China Health and Retirement Longitudinal Study by the Peking University Biomedical Ethics Committee.

This study used de-identified data obtained through each cohort’s official data-access procedures and required no additional ethical approval.

Power Analysis Check

No power analyses were detected.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.
