Northern Ireland (NICOLA-HCAP, n = 1,037) gives 6.2%; the HCAP-network preprint gives 7.5% and the ELSA-380 HCAP project report 13%, which would move the range to 1.43 and 1.86 respectively. All England values are algorithmic classifications rather than clinical adjudications. What differs between these populations, then, is more plausibly a property of the measurement than of the disease, and no recalibration within a single metric can repair it.

Comparison with previous work

Our results extend three earlier observations into a quantitative statement. First, item-level analyses of harmonised cognitive batteries have shown that individual items discriminate differently across countries [9]; we show that this differential functioning is large enough to reject scalar invariance and, more importantly, to render the resulting prevalence ratio unidentifiable. Second, unsupervised approaches -clustering of harmonised batteries [10, 11] and latent class models, as fitted here -produce estimates that differ substantially from cut-off-based ones, and our three-indicator designs span 1.89-to 3.67-fold ranges depending on design. Third, the recent European analysis of under-diagnosis in SHARE [23] documented that most people meeting algorithmic criteria lack a diagnosis; we find that the ratio of diagnosis rates between algorithm-positive and algorithm-negative respondents ranges from 22.3 in England to 1.6 in China, implying that the algorithm's meaning -not merely its calibration -varies across settings.

A parallel literature has attempted to make these numbers comparable by other means.

Unsupervised clustering of harmonised batteries produced global estimates several-fold above adjudicated ones in some settings [11]; retraining a clinical algorithm in a single reference sample [24] improves performance within that sample but does not establish that the retrained coefficients transport. Network-level work has instead emphasised protocol design and data-collection quality [25, 26], and a construct-validity check using age gradients concluded that harmonised scores measure a broadly similar construct across six countries [27]. Our findings are consistent with that conclusion and locate the failure one level lower.

Agreement on rank order and on the direction of age gradients [27] does not imply agreement on intercepts, and prevalence is an intercept quantity: it asks how many people fall below a fixed threshold, not whether they are ordered in the same way. Treating cross-cohort differences in that quantity as cohort effects, as recent generation comparisons have done The comparison with standardised clinical assessment is the most informative. Across the three populations in which both approaches can be applied (USA, Europe and England), harmonised-protocol prevalence lies between 7.0% and 10.0% -a 1.4-fold spread; across all five published harmonised-protocol estimates it spans 6.2% to 10.0%, or 1.6-fold [12, [16] [17] [18] [19].

Our brief algorithm, applied to the same populations, spans 4.3-fold, and 10.2-fold once China is included. The difference between the two is attributable to the measurement protocol, and it is larger than the real cross-national variation that GBD models across 204 countries (2.4-fold) [2]. The British case is the clearest: an algorithm-based estimate of 1.25% against a harmonised-protocol estimate of 7.06% that we derived from the same survey's HCAP subsample, and against 6.2% in neighbouring Northern Ireland, 7.5% in an HCAPnetwork preprint and 13% in the project report -a five-to ten-fold discrepancy whichever value is used, in a country with a well-resourced survey.

Interpretation: the label, the ordering and the risk

It is tempting to conclude that brief algorithms are simply invalid and should be abandoned.

That would discard what they do carry. Relative risk is broadly portable: the mortality hazard ratio attached to the algorithmic label was 2.20, 2.27 and 1.83 across three countries, with a pooled estimate of 2.14; formal heterogeneity was absent under the pre-specified randomeffects model (I² = 0, p = 0.40) though interaction tests ranged from p = 0.004 to p = 0.47 across specifications. Rank ordering is also more stable than level but not invariant: China is highest and England lowest in 42% of the 384 four-cohort specifications (50% of those with an estimable ratio), and the four structured analyses we report (equating, latent class, imputation, standardisation) each preserved that ordering. What is not portable is the absolute number, the absolute risk attached to it, and therefore any policy target expressed as a count of expected cases. These four cohorts are probability samples of community-dwelling adults aged ≥65 in their settings, so they exclude nursing-home residents and cannot be extrapolated to clinical or hospital populations, nor to low-and middle-income countries other than China.

This suggests a sharp distinction that the field has not made: prevalence as a label (how many people meet a criterion) and prevalence as a quantity (how many people have the disease) behave differently. Labels are cheap, reproducible within a cohort and useful for relative comparisons. Quantities are what GBD, WHO targets and national plans require, and they cannot be recovered from a label unless the label's operating characteristics are known in that population -which is precisely what our impossibility result shows cannot be assumed.

Implications for reporting and for global burden estimates

We propose a seven-item minimum reporting standard for cross-national prevalence work (Table 4), of which the first three are cheap enough to be adopted immediately; four of the The same logic applies to inequality. Educational gradients in the dementia label were steep and consistent in direction (low education 5.6-9.2 times high education, excluding China, where no high-education respondent met the cut-off; concentration indices -0.12 to -0.42), but their magnitude and their rank order across cohorts changed completely when the definition changed: under self-reported physician diagnosis the low-high gap fell to 1.3-4.9 percentage points in the high-income cohorts and reversed to -6.4 percentage points in China, and China moved from first to last ( S12 Table, S1 Fig.

). Reporting standards for inequality should therefore be as explicit as those for prevalence: which definition, which gradient metric, and whether the ordering is stable across definitions.

Applied to global burden, these standards change the headline. Re-scaling the GBD 2021 total by our algorithm-based estimates alone yields anything between roughly 9 and 80 million cases, a range an order of magnitude wider than the model's own uncertainty interval.

The HCAP-consistent ratios, by contrast, are close to unity, which localises the problem in the measurement instrument rather than in the global model. The policy consequence is not that the global estimate is wrong, but that its principal uncertainty is unmeasured and currently unacknowledged: a country whose survey uses a brief battery can move its apparent burden several-fold by changing an item's scoring.

Limitations

First, the English harmonised-protocol estimate is our own algorithmic classification of the ELSA-HCAP sample, not a clinical adjudication of the kind underlying the US figure (10.0%).

It agrees with the unpublished English value (7.5%) and with Northern Ireland (6.2%) and is reproducible from the released data, but its absolute level could differ from an adjudicated value by several percentage points: in the Mex-Cog validation the same pipeline returned 4.0% against a published 6.7%, and 5.2% after an informant-threshold adjustment, so the English estimate may be biased downward; if it is, by roughly the 2.7-point shortfall seen in Mex-Cog, the English harmonised-protocol value would be about 9.7% and the under-detection ratio about 7.8 rather than 5.7 -a correction that strengthens rather than weakens the conclusion while making the 1.4-fold harmonised-protocol spread even narrower. The under-detection conclusion nonetheless holds whichever English value is used (ratios 4.96-10.4); the exact magnitude does not. One further data caveat: the ELSA age variable is about three years behind the wave-10 interview; re-computing the English estimate with the wave-10 age variable gives 1.07% rather than 1.25% and raises the English-European contrast to 4.9-fold (3.4-7.2), so the age handling makes our estimates conservative. Second, no published CHARLS-HCAP prevalence exists, so the harmonisedprotocol comparison covers three of our four cohorts; the Chinese arm of the impossibility argument therefore rests on a national door-to-door survey rather than on HCAP, although the conclusion is robust across a grid of plausible true values. Third, CHARLS does not administer backward counting, so two points were imputed for all Chinese respondents; because the item carries almost no information anywhere (loadings 0.02-0.09) this is unlikely to affect the conclusions, but it does mean that the Chinese score is a three-item scale in practice. Fourth, the proxy branch in SHARE was calibrated rather than directly measured, and sensitivity analyses varying proxy prevalence by ±50% moved the SHARE estimate only between 5.04% and 5.48% (and extreme assumptions in which proxies carry the same or 2.2 times the self-report prevalence moved it to 4.88-4.95%), so this assumption does not drive the cross-national comparison. Fifth, equated prevalence values are model-based counterfactuals, not measurements, and the equating models fit only moderately well (CFI 0.884-0.924; RMSEA 0.097-0.185); we therefore report equating as one line of evidence among several rather than as the primary estimate. Sixth, we analyse prevalence crosssectionally and cannot address incidence, and ELSA does not release death status in the relevant waves, so the mortality anchor rests on three cohorts. Seventh, differential item functioning and genuine population differences in the latent trait are not separately identified without external anchors; our partial-scalar solution provides a lower bound on the measurement contribution, not an exact decomposition. Eighth, misclassification risk in algorithmic classification is not random: it has been shown to vary systematically with race and ethnicity in the USA [29], and our own results show the same for education, so the country-level bias we document is likely to be heterogeneous within countries as well.

Conclusion

Across four population-based cohorts, the same brief dementia algorithm yields prevalence estimates that differ tenfold, but the difference is not identifiable as disease: it depends on analytical choices, on a single poorly functioning item, and on operating characteristics that cannot be simultaneously true in the USA and China. Standardised clinical assessment narrows the same comparison to about 1.4-fold, and to no more than 1.9-fold under any available English estimate. Cross-national dementia prevalence should therefore be reported as a specification range or an equated interval, with an explicit invariance statement and an enrichment ratio -not as a single number. What remains more stable is the partial ordering of populations and, more clearly, the relative risk attached to the label; what does not is the count, and therefore the burden, on which policy currently relies.

[

28], therefore risks attributing to ageing what belongs to the instrument.

seven are summarised here. (i) Construct specification: state which items, which cut-off, whether the cut-off is absolute or relative, and evidence of measurement invariance, or an explicit statement that the comparison assumes invariance. (ii) Enrichment ratio: report the ratio of diagnosis rates between algorithm-positive and algorithm-negative respondents -a minimal, cheap check of whether the label means the same thing in each setting (7.0-22.3 in the high-income cohorts versus 1.6 in China). (iii) Specification range or equated interval: report the median and interquartile range of the prevalence ratio across a pre-specified set of defensible specifications, rather than a single point estimate; and (iv) anchoring bound: the implied operating characteristics when an external gold standard exists, with an explicit statement when no admissible solution exists.

No single pair of sensitivity and specificity can reproduce both the US gold-standard prevalence (10.0%) and the Chinese national survey estimate (6.0%) while also reproducing what the algorithm actually returns in each country: the implied value of Se+Sp-1 is -1.86, outside the interval [-1, 1] in which any screening test must live.
